11

12

13

14

15 
3.6 million customers in New York, Massachusetts, and Rhode Island.   
16 
 
17

18 
York that provide retail gas service to more than 2.4 million customers in 
19 
three service territories: Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National 
20 
Grid (“Niagara Mohawk”) serves areas of eastern and central New York; The 
21

3

4

5 
gas networks, including emergency response, gas engineering, construction 
6

II. 
Purpose of Testimony 
13 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel’s                
14 
testimony? 
15 
A. 
The purpose of the Panel’s testimony is to provide a forecast of the capital 
16 
investments of the Company for the twelve-month period ending March 31, 
17 
2021 (“Rate Year”), and the three subsequent twelve-month periods ending 
18 
March 31, 2022 (“Data Year 1”), March 31, 2023 (“Data Year 2”), and March 
19 
31, 2024 (“Data Year 3”) (Data Year 1, Data Year 2, and Data Year 3 are 
20 
collectively referred to as the “Data Years”).  The Panel also presents a 
21 


--- Page 10 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 7 of 110 
forecast for the fifteen-month period (“Gap Period”) between December 31, 
1 
2018, which is the end of the Company’s Historic Test Year, and the 
2 
beginning of the Rate Year.    
3 
 
4 
The Panel discusses direct capital expenditures that will (i) increase the safety 
5 
and reliability of the Company’s gas network, (ii) modernize the Company’s 
6 
gas transmission and distribution infrastructure to reduce emissions while 
7 
continuing to meet customer needs, and (iii) enhance resiliency and the 
8 
Company’s ability to respond to future weather events.  The Panel will also 
9 
discuss the Company’s practices and policies for maximizing the efficiency of 
10 
its capital delivery program from planning and budgeting through the 
11 
completion of construction.     
12 
 
The Panel’s testimony provides an overview of the significant projects in the 
13 
Company’s capital plan, including retirement of leak prone pipe (“LPP”) and 
14 
new safety programs to identify and address system risks.  The Panel’s 
15 
testimony also presents an overview of the Company’s pipeline integrity and 
16 
reliability programs that will improve the overall safety and reliability of the 
17 
Company’s gas system, and will also address recently enacted, as well as 
18 
pending, pipeline safety regulations administered by the U.S. Department of 
19 
Transportation 
(“DOT”), 
Pipeline 
and 
Hazardous 
Materials 
Safety 
20 
Administration (“PHMSA”).  The Panel discusses the Company’s plans and 
21 


--- Page 11 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 8 of 110 
infrastructure projects necessary to ensure that the Company can reliably serve 
1 
its existing customers as well as forecast demand for new customer 
2 
connections within the Company’s operating territory. 
3 
 
 
4 
 
The Panel also presents the Company’s indirect capital investments, including 
5 
supply chain, inventory management/investment recovery (“IM/IR”), and fleet 
6 
investments.  The Company is also proposing significant investments in 
7 
facilities during the Rate Year and Data Years that are necessary to: support 
8 
the Company’s O&M and gas safety programs; make needed upgrades to 
9 
aging facilities; and create more modern and efficient customer support and 
10 
employee work spaces.   
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Does the Panel’s testimony also address the Company’s operations and 
13 
maintenance (“O&M”) programs? 
14 
A. 
Yes.  In addition to capital investments in gas infrastructure, the Panel 
15 
describes incremental labor (full time equivalent positions or “FTEs”) and 
16 
non-labor O&M expenses that the Company proposes in the Rate Year and 
17 
Data Years, the costs of which are not fully reflected in the twelve-month 
18 
period beginning January 1, 2018 and ending December 31, 2018 (“Historic 
19 
Test Year”).  These expenses represent known and measurable changes from 
20 
Historic Test Year expenses that are necessary to (i) improve system safety 
21

and reliability, (ii) address new and emerging safety regulations, (iii) enhance

2

3 
FTEs. 
4

Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel

1 
III. 
Capital and O&M Plan Objectives and Priorities 
2 
A. Plan Objectives 
3 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s overall objective of its infrastructure and 
4 
operations programs. 
5 
A. 
The Company designs its gas infrastructure and operations programs to 
6 
provide safe and reliable gas delivery service to customers at reasonable costs.  
7 
Over the last several years, the Company has significantly increased 
8 
investment to modernize and enhance the resiliency of its gas assets.   
9 
 
10 
Significant capital investment over the next several years is required to ensure 
11 
that the gas system continues to operate safely while meeting the demands of 
12 
customers and to enable further modernization of the Company’s facilities and 
13 
operations in a manner that achieves meaningful methane emissions 
14 
reductions in furtherance of the Company’s Future of Heat strategy.  The 
15 
proposed plan includes capital and O&M spending to meet both customer 
16 
needs and satisfy state and federal regulatory requirements and goals, 
17 
including retirement of LPP, gas pipeline safety enhancements, and 
18 
deployment of non-pipeline alternatives.  While significant investments are 
19 
required to meet these needs and objectives, in developing its capital and 
20 
O&M plans, the Company balanced the need for spending to achieve safety 
21 


--- Page 16 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 13 of 110 
and service objectives with the need to manage costs and minimize impacts on 
1 
customer rates.     
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What are the Company’s projected direct and indirect capital forecasts? 
4 
A. 
A summary of the Company’s total capital plan forecast for the Rate Year and 
5 
Data Years is provided in Table 1. 
6 
Table 1:  Indirect and Direct Capital Plan

9 
Q. 
Why is the scale of Company’s capital investment plan increasing? 
10 
A. 
Several developments have required that KEDLI and other natural gas 
11 
distribution utilities increase their annual capital expenditures.  Notably, 
12 
pipeline safety incidents, such as the tragic events in San Bruno, California, 
13 
Allentown, Pennsylvania, and East Harlem, New York, and the more recent 
14 
incident in Merrimack Valley, Massachusetts, have appropriately increased 
15 
focus on pipeline safety and the need to carefully monitor and replace aging 
16 
pipeline infrastructure.  Weather events such as Superstorm Sandy and the 
17 
Polar Vortex, and the expectation that similar events will occur in the future, 
18 
require the Company to find ways to protect its facilities from severe weather.   
19 
 
20 


--- Page 17 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 14 of 110 
In response to these events, the industry has adopted new safety requirements 
1 
and is implementing best practices to modernize and protect gas facilities.  For 
2 
example, since the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, (i) new 
3 
Commission safety regulations and directives now require additional safety 
4 
inspections of inside service lines and plastic fusions; (ii) PHMSA is 
5 
implementing new safety requirements related to transmission system 
6 
integrity management and verification this year; (iii) and the New York State 
7 
Legislature is currently considering a bill that would require licensed 
8 
professional engineers (“PEs”) to review and certify gas facility design plans.  
9 
The Commission and utility industry review of recent incidents has also 
10 
resulted in recommendations for safety best practices, including overpressure 
11 
protection improvements and enhanced contractor oversight.  These mandated 
12 
and recommended improvements require significant additional investments 
13 
and compliance resources. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
How will the Company support the increased level of capital investment? 
16 
A. 
As the Company developed plans to modernize its gas assets, it also began to 
17 
build and enhance its operations, engineering, resource planning, work 
18 
management, and quality control organizations and capabilities to deliver 
19 
increasing levels of capital investment.  The Company will continue to 
20 
develop these capabilities in the Rate Year and Data Years by adding 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 15 of 110 
incremental labor resources to execute the capital plan and support the 
1 
increased operations workload (discussed in Section VI).  The capital 
2 
investment plan includes additional field training resources, facilities, and 
3 
tools to enhance the development of the internal work force.  
4 
 
5 
Regarding contractor resources, over the past several years, the Company 
6 
developed a procurement strategy that supports sustainable growth in 
7 
qualified contractors to meet the work plan increases.  To ensure adequate 
8 
levels of qualified, skilled labor and the challenges around developing 
9 
qualified contractors, the Company has worked to:  
10 
• Establish longer term contracts to enable contractors to plan and invest 
11 
in hiring, training, facilities, and equipment to meet the Company’s 
12 
construction needs  
13 
• Provide greater work plan visibility to contractors on forecast crew 
14 
requirements which will enable them to develop the required capacity 
15 
• Manage the work plan to limit seasonal variability to support a stable 
16 
contractor workforce and promote worker retention 
17 
• Continue partnerships with community colleges, trade schools, and 
18 
veterans group to attract and develop new sources of skilled labor to 
19 
build the workforce  
20 
 
21 


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 16 of 110 
Q. 
Does the Company require additional personnel in the Rate Year and 
1 
Data Years to execute its capital and O&M programs? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  The Company forecasts the need for an additional 135.3 FTE positions 
3 
by Data Year 3 to support the additional capital investment, increasing O&M 
4 
workload due to new and pending regulatory requirements, new programs 
5 
discussed below, and the gas safety programs presented in the Gas Safety 
6 
Panel Testimony.  These FTEs include positions in contractor oversight, gas 
7 
pipeline 
safety 
management, 
corrosion 
control, 
field 
operations, 
8 
instrumentation and regulation (“I&R”), gas control, gas system engineering, 
9 
and gas asset management.   The cost of these FTEs will be charged to both 
10 
capital and O&M programs based on the job function and nature of the work.  
11 
Exhibit __ (GIOP-8) identifies the incremental FTE positions by function.  
12 
KEDLI’s Revenue Requirements Panel presents the labor O&M associated 
13 
with these FTEs.   
14 
 
15 
B. Cost Pressures and Cost Mitigation 
16 
Q. 
What explains the significant increases reflected in the investment plan?  
17 
A. 
The Company’s investment plan reflects increases in both the scope of work 
18 
(units) and the costs elements to perform this work (unit costs).  As discussed 
19 
above, there are several factors that are collectively increasing the size of the 
20 
Company’s investment plan over the next several years, including incremental 
21 


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 17 of 110 
work driven by federal and state regulatory mandates and the need to upgrade 
1 
aged infrastructure and replace dated facilities.  At the same time, the 
2 
Company is experiencing increasing unit costs resulting from new work 
3 
requirements and enhanced safety practices, and the rising costs of materials 
4 
and contractors, in part due to increasing demands for these resources across 
5 
the industry.  
6 
 
7 
Indeed, a primary driver of increases in the capital plan is significantly higher 
8 
unit costs for main installation work across multiple mandated programs, 
9 
including the proactive and reactive main replacement, customer connections, 
10 
corrosion, and city/state construction (“CSC”) programs.   
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What factors are contributing to the Company’s unit cost increases?   
13 
A. 
Exhibit __ (GIOP-2) shows the components that have contributed to the main 
14 
installation unit cost increases.  The factors contributing to an increase in the 
15 
Company’s main replacement unit cost include: 
16 
• Restoration Costs: increasing municipal restoration requirements and 
17 
significantly increasing costs for dumping excavation spoils (more 
18 
stringent environmental regulations has led to a reduction in dumping 
19 
locations and higher costs) 
20 


--- Page 21 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 18 of 110 
• Services: work scope includes mains with higher service density (more 
1 
services per mile of main) than in prior years 
2 
• Test Holes: process safety improvements requiring stricter procedures 
3 
related to test holes 
4 
• Cross Bores: process safety improvements requiring more camera 
5 
scoping during HDD in sewer districts

17 
i. 
Proactive Main Replacement (LPP) Program 
18 
Q. 
Please describe the inventory of LPP existing on the Company’s system.   
19 
A. 
LPP in KEDLI’s service territory is comprised of twelve inch and smaller 
20 
main and associated services that are: (i) unprotected (i.e., non-cathodically 
21 


--- Page 29 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 26 of 110 
protected) steel pipe (whether bare or coated), (ii) cast and wrought iron pipe, 
1 
(iii) pre-1985 vintage Aldyl-A plastic pipe, (iv) unprotected steel/wrought 
2 
iron, copper services, and (v) pre-1974 High Density Polyethylene (“HDPE”) 
3 
and pre-1985 Aldyl-A plastic services.  As of the end of CY 2018, the 
4 
Company has approximately 3,455 miles of LPP in its remaining inventory. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
What is the Company’s proposal regarding retirement of LPP? 
7 
A. 
To reduce the risk of leaks and breaks, improve system safety, performance 
8 
and reliability, meet the Company’s commitment to enhance customer 
9 
satisfaction and reduce methane emissions, the Company has prioritized the 
10 
retirement of older and higher-risk gas infrastructure – specifically, LPP and 
11 
associated services – that disproportionally contributes to leaks on KEDLI’s 
12 
system.   
13 
 
14 
 
The Company’s current plan funds LPP replacements of 115 miles in CY 
15 
2017, 135 miles in CY 2018, and 155 miles in CY 2019.  The Company 
16 
proposes to continue its current pace of LPP retirement of 155 miles per year 
17 
beginning in CY 2020, with a cumulative total of at least 620 miles from 
18 
CY20 to CY23.  The performance metrics and targets for LPP replacements 
19 
are presented in detail in KEDNY and KEDLI’s Gas Safety Panel testimony.  
20 


--- Page 30 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 27 of 110 
The proposed LPP retirement rate will position the Company to fully retire its 
1 
remaining LPP inventory in 20 years.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Why is the Company proposing to continue the current pace of LPP 
4 
retirement through Data Year 3? 
5 
A. 
As the Company has worked over the last several years to retire LPP, the 
6 
Company observed a leveling-off of the overall leak rate, as shown in Table 2.  
7 
While leak rates on LPP still outpace the leak rate for overall distribution 
8 
piping, the leak rates have not continued to rise significantly over the past 
9 
several years.  The current leak rate for all distribution piping is 0.15 leaks per 
10 
mile.  The 2018 leak rate for LPP was 0.31 leaks per mile. 
11 
12 


--- Page 31 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 28 of 110 
Table 2:  KEDLI Historic Leak Rate 
1 
 
2 
Because the leak rates have stabilized to a lower range for nearly a decade, 
3 
KEDLI’s proposal enables the Company to moderate the overall cost of the 
4 
LPP retirement program in the near term while still reaching the goals of the 
5 
program over the next 20 years.  Exhibit __ (GIOP-4) provides projected leak 
6 
rates for LPP for various main replacement strategies. 
7 
 
8

9 
A. 
Yes.  As is noted in the Company’s Future of Heat Panel testimony, KEDNY 
10 
and KEDLI are establishing a target to reduce methane emissions from the gas 
11 
distribution system 60 percent (from 1990 levels) by 2035, and retirement of 
12 
LPP is a major component toward reaching this ambitious goal.  Specifically, 
13

1

2 
of pipe segments with Type 3 leaks and service leaks when other risk factors

4

Q.

7

15 
 
16 
Q. 
How does the Company prioritize the retirement of main segments for 
17 
the Proactive Main Replacement Program? 
18 
A. 
Each year, the Company prioritizes retirement of LPP segments by using a 
19 
risk ranking algorithm that is part of the Company’s Distribution Integrity 
20 
Management Plan (“DIMP”) and the Company’s Gas Operating Procedure for 
21 


--- Page 34 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 31 of 110 
the Identification, Evaluation and Prioritization of Distribution Main 
1 
Segments for Replacement (ENG04030).  The Company’s risk model 
2 
calculates a relative risk score for each LPP segment based on specific 
3 
performance data and localized incident probabilities and consequences, 
4 
combined with calculated risk factors for the asset classes under evaluation.  
5 
This risk-based algorithm, along with the Company’s engineering judgment, 
6 
which takes all factors and risks into consideration in each case, form the 
7 
foundation of the LPP retirement strategy.  The Company is proposing to 
8 
target more cast iron main for retirement, where possible, in consideration of 
9 
other risk factors, due to increased age and risk of this material.  Additionally, 
10 
as a result of the Storm Hardening Collaborative following the 2016 KEDNY 
11 
and KEDLI Rate Cases, the Company added flood zone factors to its risk 
12 
ranking algorithm such that where other risk factors are equal, the Company 
13 
will target LPP retirements in Federal Emergency Management Agency 
14 
(“FEMA”) 100 and 500-year flood zones.   
15

7 
ii. 
Gas Safety Integrity Management and Integrity Verification:  
8 
IMP and IVP Programs 
9 
Q. 
What is the Company’s IMP? 
10 
A. 
The Company’s transmission pipeline IMP is a safety program mandated by 
11 
the Pipeline Safety Improvement Act of 2002 and corresponding DOT 
12 
regulations.  The IMP identifies and addresses potential issues affecting the 
13 
physical soundness of Company facilities before they become safety or 
14 
performance issues.  The Company conducts baseline and periodic 
15 
reassessments of transmission facilities to identify and evaluate potential 
16 
threats to “Covered Segments” of pipelines, i.e., transmission pipelines that 
17 
could affect High Consequence Areas (areas where a pipeline failure could 
18 
have significant adverse consequences), as well as remediation of significant 
19 
defects discovered during such assessments.  In regions of the United States 
20 


--- Page 38 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 35 of 110 
where older gas distribution systems are common, IMPs have become a key 
1 
component of gas pipeline safety through improving pipeline integrity.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe the IMP capital investments.  
4 
A. 
Currently, IMP assessments may be accomplished through either external 
5 
corrosion direct assessment (“ECDA”) or in-line inspection (“ILI”) 
6 
technology, which uses a robotic device known as a “pig” to evaluate the 
7 
condition of the inside of the pipeline.  ILI is considered superior to ECDA 
8 
because of the ability to identify condition problems originating internally.  
9 
IMP investments are necessary to: (i) support in-line inspections (e.g., 
10 
installation 
of 
pig 
launchers 
and 
receivers, 
and 
pipe 
11 
reconfiguration/replacement), and (ii) resolve issues discovered during 
12 
pipeline inspections conducted through either method.   
13 
 
14 
The construction activities associated with these expenditures involve the 
15 
installation of “hot tap” fittings, the reconfiguration of such fittings to allow 
16 
ILI passage, the construction of access points to allow tethered in-line 
17 
inspection and, in some cases, the replacement of pipeline segments. 
18 
 
19 
Q. 
Please describe the IMP capital investment needs for the Rate Year and 
20 
Data Years. 
21 


--- Page 39 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 36 of 110 
Currently, 21 percent of the Company’s DOT pipeline is ILI enabled, 
1 
increased from 17 percent when the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases 
2 
was filed.  Over the long term, the Company’s plan is to reach 80 percent ILI 
3 
enablement of the Company’s DOT piping.  The Company’s IMP capital 
4 
investments in the Rate Year and Data Years will drive toward this goal and 
5 
will significantly improve the Company’s ability to identify integrity issues.  
6 
 
7 
Q. 
How are the IMP workplan and associated IMP capital investment 
8 
forecast determined?  
9 
A. 
The workplan began in 2002 based on initial assessments using the 
10 
Company’s existing risk model.  Segments were prioritized for ILI 
11 
enablement over a multi-year workplan based on a combination of their risk 
12 
score and other relevant factors, such as facility characteristics or geography, 
13 
to determine what work to do when.  Some segments must be re-assessed in 
14 
certain years according to when they were initially assessed.  The workplan is 
15 
updated annually and as required assessments are completed.  Additionally, 
16 
ILI projects typically consist of two years of design and procurement work 
17 
followed by construction in the third year.   
18 
 
19 
IMP spending can fluctuate significantly year to year depending on the 
20 
projects identified for inclusion in the workplan.  Because the work must be 
21 


--- Page 40 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 37 of 110 
done by segment, the length, geographic location, and characteristics of the 
1 
segments that need to be addressed each year contribute to different costs such 
2 
that historic program costs are not indicative of future program needs.  Once 
3 
segments to be addressed by the workplan are identified, cost forecasts are 
4 
level one project estimates based on prior projects of similar size and scope 
5 
adjusted for known variables.  Additionally, some larger projects in the IMP 
6 
are separately budgeted on the capital investment plan.       
7 
 
8 
The IMP workplan for the Rate Year and Data Years is described in more 
9 
detail in the IMP data sheet in Exhibit __ (GIOP-5).  The IMP capital 
10 
investments required to address known risks identified via assessments and to 
11 
increase ILI enablement are approximately $7.4 million in the Rate Year, $7.4 
12 
million in Data Year 1, $7.0 million in Data Year 2, and $7.0 million in Data 
13 
Year 3.   
14 
 
15 
Q. 
What is the status of the pending federal regulations in this area?   
16 
A. 
In May 2016, PHMSA issued a Notice of Proposed Rulemaking (“NPRM”) 
17 
that proposes new pipeline safety regulations that include a requirement for 
18 
increased inspection of IMP-covered pipelines utilizing ILI technology and an 
19 
expanded definition of High Consequence Areas, as well as a host of other 
20 


--- Page 41 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 38 of 110 
requirements.  To meet these requirements, transmission pipelines must be ILI 
1 
enabled.  
2 
  
3 
The Company expects that regulations will become effective in the fall of CY 
4 
2019, and an implementation schedule will be included.  Because the 
5 
Company’s IMP workplan is risk based, and in light of PHMSA’s new 
6 
regulations expanding IMP, the Company believes that its proposed IMP 
7 
program is a reasonable and conservative approach to managing pipeline 
8 
integrity during the Rate Year and Data Years.    
9 
 
10 
Q. 
What is covered in the Company’s IVP Program? 
11 
A. 
The Pipeline Safety Act of 2011 also mandates that PHMSA establish rules 
12 
requiring operators to demonstrate their pipelines are “fit for service” by 
13 
reviewing construction records for each pipeline segment to confirm it is 
14 
operating within design parameters.  The May 2016 NPRM also proposes new 
15 
rules regarding the maximum allowable operating pressure (“MAOP”) and 
16 
pressure testing requirements for existing pipelines, including (i) eliminating 
17 
the exemption for establishing the MAOP of pre-1970 “grandfathered” pipe 
18 
segments; (ii) mandating additional pressure testing or replacement for 
19 
pipelines without adequate pressure test records; and (iii) requiring operators 
20 
who lack certain records to establish material properties using approved 
21 


--- Page 42 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 39 of 110 
methods (e.g., cutting and testing pipe samples).  In advance of a final 
1 
rulemaking, PHMSA issued an advisory bulletin (ADB-11-01) directing 
2 
operators to perform a detailed threat and risk analysis that includes a records 
3 
review of their systems.   
4 
 
5 
The Company’s IVP program began in 2011 and includes thorough record 
6 
reviews, pipeline replacement, and retirement of non-essential pipeline 
7 
segments.  As with the IMP, the IVP is primarily based on the Company’s 
8 
assessment of system risks, while also incorporating PHMSA’s proposed 
9 
rulemaking.   
10 
 
11 
Q 
What is the status of the Company’s IVP records review and its IVP 
12 
Program proposal for the Rate Year and Data Years? 
13 
A. 
Through its IVP to date, KEDLI has completed the MAOP records review on 
14 
100 percent of its DOT-jurisdictional pipelines.  Going forward, the IVP 
15 
addresses transmission main replacements and testing necessitated by 
16 
incomplete records identified by the review and pressure testing and preparing 
17 
to comply with additional testing that will be required by the new rules.  
18 
Where the Company has identified incomplete records, pipelines will be 
19 
replaced, or records will be recreated through testing.  This work is necessary 
20 
to ensure system integrity regardless of PHMSA’s proposed requirements.  
21 


--- Page 43 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 40 of 110 
The IVP work plan is described in more detail in the IVP data sheet in Exhibit 
1 
__ (GIOP-5) and is approximately $0.8 million in the Rate Year, $3.0 million 
2 
in Data Year 1, $6.5M in Data Year 2, and $29.5 million in Data Year 3. 
3 
 
4 
iii. 
Transmission Station Integrity Program 
5 
Q. 
Please describe the Transmission Station Integrity Program. 
6 
A. 
This program is similar to the Company’s IVP, but the program addresses 
7 
transmission-connected pressure regulating stations and take stations 
8 
(“transmission stations”).  PHMSA’s 2016 NPRM, related advisory bulletins, 
9 
and discussions between PHMSA and industry stakeholders during the NPRM 
10 
process have indicated that the proposed rules will apply to transmission 
11 
stations.  Therefore, the Company is proposing a program for IVP records 
12 
review and, where records are inadequate to demonstrate that transmission 
13 
station facilities are fit for service, capital investments for station 
14 
rehabilitation or partial or full station replacements.  Similar to the IMP and 
15 
IVP Programs for transmission pipelines, these investments are prudent in 
16 
advance of the final rulemaking to address risk presented by older pressure 
17 
regulating facilities for which there are inadequate records.  The Company is 
18 
beginning to ramp-up this program in FY 2020, and reaches full 
19 
implementation in Data Year 2, and the spending is expected will levelize 
20 
year to year after Data Year 2.  The forecast for this program is based on the 
21 


--- Page 44 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 41 of 110 
number and vintage of transmission stations the program is expected to 
1 
address and level one estimates of similar station project rehabilitation and 
2 
replacement projects historically.  The capital investments required for this 
3 
program are $3.0 million in the Rate Year, $15.0 million in Data Year 1, 
4 
$19.4 million in Data Year 2, and $19.8 million in Data Year 3.

iv. 
Southeast Suffolk Project 
7 
Q. 
Please describe the Southeast Suffolk Project. 
8 
A. 
The Southeast Suffolk Project is a reliability project to address system needs 
9 
in the eastern region of KEDLI’s service area east and south of Riverhead, 
10 
New York.  The Company’s system in that area is operating at close to 
11 
capacity, and the Company expects increasing demand in the area over the 
12 
next five years.  Absent this project, the Company may need to implement a 
13 
moratorium for gas sales in the limited area east and south of Riverhead, New 
14 
York.  This project is required to address system needs and avoid a limited 
15 
moratorium regardless of whether the NESE project is approved.  The project 
16 
will install approximately 8,500 feet of 16-inch, 350 psig transmission main, a 
17 
new regulator station, and 5,800 feet of 12-inch steel distribution main.  The 
18 
Company’s capital investment plan includes approximately $20 million in the 
19 
Rate Year, $21.6 million in Data Year 1, $0.1 million in Data Year 2, and $0.3 
20 
million in Data Year 3.

v. 
Northwest Nassau Project 
2 
Q. 
Please describe the Northwest Nassau Transmission Project. 
3 
A. 
This three-phase transmission reliability project was included in the 2016 
4

5

6

7

8

9 
indicated that replacement and down rating of these three transmission 
10 
pipelines will mitigate system risks, increase reliability, resolve low pressure 
11 
issues, and position the gas transmission system to accommodate a future 
12 
supply point in that area.  When all phases are complete, the project will 
13 
replace 8.8 miles of transmission main, install 5.6 miles of new pipe, down 
14 
rate 9.1 miles of pipe and construct two new regulator stations.  Phase One of 
15 
the project has been completed.  As originally proposed in 2016, the entire 
16 
project was expected to be complete by the end of Fiscal year 2023, but the 
17 
project schedule has slipped into Fiscal Year 2024 due to municipal 
18 
permitting delays.  To complete Phases Two and Three, the Company’s 
19

20

vi. 
Renewable Natural Gas (“RNG”) Interconnections Program  
4 
Q. 
Please describe KEDLI’s new RNG Interconnections Program. 
5 
A. 
This is an innovative program that is part of the Company’s overall strategy to 
6 
encourage interconnection of third-party RNG projects to KEDLI’s natural 
7 
gas distribution system. High interconnection costs have proven to be a 
8 
primary impediment to the development and interconnection of new RNG 
9 
projects in KEDLI’s service territory.  Through this program, the Company 
10 
will install and maintain some of the necessary interconnection equipment, 
11 
specifically meters, analyzers, and odorization equipment, that currently is the 
12 
sole financial and operational responsibility of project developers, thus 
13 
lowering interconnection costs.  This program complements the Company’s 
14 
development of a standard RNG project interconnection agreement that 
15 
resulted from the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, as well as the 
16 
Company’s proposals toward decarbonizing the gas network (e.g., a new 
17 
green gas tariff offering), as discussed in the Future of Heat Panel’s testimony. 
18 
19

Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel

1

3

4

5 
is likely to reach maturity to the interconnection phase during the Rate Year 
6

7 
based on the cost of meters, analyzers, and odorization equipment for one 
8

9 
provided in the data sheet in Exhibit __ (GIOP-5).

Q. 
What types of activities are associated with cost of removal? 
16

17 
removed from the asset inventory, whether a different asset is added in its 
18 
place.  This type of work would include, but is not limited to, the activities 
19 
associated with disconnection, removal and disposal (or retirement in place) 
20 
of gas mains, gas services and related facilities.

Q. 
Please describe some of the technologies and practices the Company uses 
14 
to reduce the total cost of its capital expenditures. 
15 
A. 
As discussed in the testimony of Company Witness Christopher J. Connolly, 
16 
National Grid has undertaken a comprehensive effort to evaluate the 
17 
technology and business processes that support its gas businesses (referred to 
18 
as the Gas Business Enablement (“GBE”) Program).  Among the core 
19 
investments of the GBE Program are new asset and work management, 
20 
scheduling, geographic information system, and field mobility solutions 
21 


--- Page 51 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 48 of 110 
designed to improve the Companies’ gas safety and operational performance.  
1 
While GBE is in process, the Company continues to employ a number of 
2 
technologies and best practices designed to increase the efficiency and reduce 
3 
the cost of its capital expenditures.  These practices include: 
4 
• Increasing the amount of planned capital work (versus reactive work). 
5 
• Increasing coordination among capital programs to increase 
6 
efficiencies (e.g., leveraging LPP opportunities).  
7 
• Installing more small-diameter, high-pressure facilities that can be 
8 
installed at lower cost. 
9 
• Using smaller excavating equipment, increasing operating efficiency 
10 
and reducing instances of damage (because of decreased size and 
11 
weight of equipment). 
12 
• Using “coring and keyhole” technology to repair existing mains.  
13 
• Enhancing contractor management. 
14 
• On-site reporting for work crews in many large construction projects.  
15 
• Continuing to deploy CIP lining (as described in more detail below). 
16 
In addition, as noted above, the Company’s Capital Delivery organization is in 
17 
the beginning stages of identifying opportunities for efficiencies that are 
18 
expected to reduce costs.

Page 49 of 110 
A. Capital Planning, Budgeting, and Sanctioning Process 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the annual development of the Company’s capital plan. 
2 
Each year, the Company develops a ten-year capital plan to achieve its 
3 
performance objectives of delivering safe and reliable service. Investment 
4 
Planning compiles proposed spending for programs and individual capital 
5 
projects. Programs and projects are categorized into one of four spending 
6 
rationales (Mandated, Customer Connections, Reliability and Non-
7 
Infrastructure).   
8 
 
9 
The Company currently uses a tool, C55 (known internally as “Copperleaf”), 
10 
for the prioritization and optimization of capital investments through a risk 
11 
scoring and prioritization methodology.  This tool uses criteria aligned to a 
12 
common scale to compare dissimilar inputs (e.g., financial and non-financial 
13 
inputs) to determine the set of investments, alternatives, and the timing of 
14 
projects that have the highest potential value.  
15 
 
16 
After the Companies rank the investments in C55, meetings are scheduled 
17 
with budget sponsors to review value models, project rankings, and various 
18 
scenarios produced through the optimization of investments to meet capital 
19 
planning targets.  Once plan targets have been approved by the Gas Business 
20 
Unit, they are submitted to Finance for review. 
21 


--- Page 53 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 50 of 110 
In late fall, the capital plan is reviewed by the New York Jurisdictional 
1 
President (Company Witness John Bruckner) and the Vice President, Finance, 
2 
New York (Company Witness David Doxsee).  The New York Jurisdictional 
3 
President reviews the overall customer, service quality and financial impacts 
4 
of the investment plan and may request changes to the level or mix of 
5 
investments.   
6 
 
7 
In early winter, the capital plan is presented to the National Grid USA Board 
8 
of Directors and the National Grid plc Executive Committee and, in early 
9 
spring, the capital portfolio is presented to the National Grid plc Board of 
10 
Directors for review and approval.   
11 
 
12 
Q.     Are there additional approvals needed before a project in the annual 
13 
capital plan may proceed? 
14 
A.     Yes.  Aside from the capital planning and budgeting process, specific 
15 
delegation of authority (“DOA”) approval must be obtained for any project in 
16 
the ten-year capital plan to proceed.  This process includes the sanctioning 
17 
documentation and review for projects over $1 million and other levels of 
18 
review for smaller projects.  Presently, all projects greater than $1 million 
19 
require some level of sanctioning documentation and review.  The U.S. 
20 
Sanctioning Committee (“USSC”) was established by the National Grid USA 
21 


--- Page 54 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 51 of 110 
Board of Directors specifically for this purpose.  Projects between $8 million 
1 
and $25 million are reviewed and approved by the USSC.  Projects between 
2 
$25 million to $203 million are reviewed by the USSC and then are forwarded 
3 
to a Senior Executive Sanctioning Committee (“SESC”) for review and 
4 
approval.  For projects between $1 million and $8 million, the USSC has 
5 
delegated review to an informal committee led by the Investment Planning 
6 
group and including, but not limited to, managers and directors from the 
7 
Regulatory, Estimating, Asset Management, and Procurement groups.  The 
8 
committee reviews and finalizes sanctioning papers for these projects at a 
9 
weekly meeting, and the committee then forwards the final sanction 
10 
documents to the executive sponsor of the project for approval and signature.  
11 
Projects less than $1 million do not require sanctioning and are approved 
12 
through a supervisory delegation of authority hierarchy based on certain 
13 
established thresholds.   
14 
 
15 
Q.    
Please explain the difference between the DOA review and approval 
16 
(sanctioning) process and the approved five-year capital plan used to 
17 
forecast the Rate Year and Data Years. 
18 
A.      The timing of the sanctioning process is not aligned with the capital planning 
19 
process used to forecast the Rate Year and Data Years for purposes of the rate 
20 
filing.  As described above, the Company develops a long-term investment 
21 


--- Page 55 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 52 of 110 
plan that is used as the basis for the forecast for the Rate Year and Data Year 
1 
proposals.  Project sanctioning, however, normally occurs immediately prior 
2 
to the FY for which the investment is planned.  For example, the FY 2020 
3 
capital plan will be formally sanctioned in early 2019.  Thus, the Company’s 
4 
currently-sanctioned or partially-sanctioned projects do not yet represent the 
5 
full capital forecast proposed in the Rate Year and Data Years. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please describe how the Company’s DIMP impacts its capital investment 
8 
planning. 
9 
A. 
The DIMP involves a risk-based assessment of the Company’s distribution 
10 
system to identify threats in seven categories: corrosion, natural forces, 
11 
excavation damage, other outside force damage, material and weld failure, 
12 
equipment failure/malfunction and inappropriate operation.  The DIMP 
13 
requires evaluation and prioritization of the risks that these threats pose, and 
14 
the implementation of measures to address the highest risks with an emphasis 
15 
on leak management, enhanced damage prevention, operator qualification to 
16 
reduce human error and system replacement.  Consistent with the DIMP, the 
17 
Company prioritizes asset replacements in its investment plan based on a risk 
18 
ranking that considers, among other things, leak repair history, types of leak, 
19 
pipe material, surrounding geography, segment length, nearby construction 
20 
activity, field conditions, customer issues, open leaks and engineering 
21 


--- Page 56 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 53 of 110 
judgment.  The risk ranking factors are carefully designed to consider known 
1 
differences in the performance of asset subclasses, extensive experience with 
2 
asset failures, current performance data for the asset subclasses for various 
3 
threat categories, and subject matter experts’ analysis and opinions on the 
4 
future performance of the assets. 
5 
 
6 
B. Customer Connections Category of Spending 
7 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Customer Connections spending 
8 
category.  
9 
A. 
The Customer Connections category of work accounts for approximately 21 
10 
percent ($113.9 million) of the total planned capital investment in the Rate 
11 
Year.   
12 
 
13 
KEDLI is required by the Public Service Law, the Commission’s regulations, 
14 
and tariff to extend service to new customers in its service territory in 
15 
proximity to the Company’s facilities, and to install new distribution main, up 
16 
to a 100-foot tariff allowance per customer, to do so.  Expanding the 
17 
availability of natural gas in KEDLI’s service territory can bring significant 
18 
economic benefits in the form of energy cost savings for customers, job 
19 
creation, and increased local tax revenues.  Moreover, there are environmental 
20 
benefits associated with conversion to natural gas from other, higher emission 
21 


--- Page 57 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 54 of 110 
fuels such as fuel oil.  To enable new customer connections, the Company 
1 
must make significant capital investments in mains, services, and system 
2 
reinforcements.  Customer Connection programs are designed to support 
3 
forecast customer demand and add new load by increasing system utilization 
4 
in a cost-effective way.  Customer Connection programs involve the 
5 
installation of new mains, services and meters and include system 
6 
reinforcement.   
7 
 
8 
The Customer Connections – Install Main, Install Services (including the 
9 
Clean Choice Program), Meter Purchases, Install Meter, and Automatic Meter 
10 
Reading (“AMR”) Programs and costs are presented in Exhibit __ (GIOP-5).   
11 
 
 
12 
Q. 
Please describe recent customer demand trends in the Company’s service 
13 
territory. 
14 
A. 
Gas price advantage is expected to increase between CY 2020 and 2023 and is 
15 
expected to drive continued demand for gas, particularly in the new 
16 
construction market.  The updated customer demand forecast shows steady 
17 
demand in the residential conversion market.  The commercial conversion 
18 
market is forecast to decrease slightly due to market saturation.  The 
19 
residential new construction market is expected to increase sharply, and multi-
20 


--- Page 58 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 55 of 110 
family new construction is expected to begin rising in CY 2020.  Non-
1 
residential construction is expected to decrease.     
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What explains the increases in the Customer Connections forecasts? 
4 
A. 
The increases are driven by higher unit costs of main and service installation 
5 
due the factors discussed in Section III, above.  While units are not expected 
6 
to increase dramatically, the cost of main installation and reinforcements to 
7 
serve new customers increased as compared to prior years.  Additionally, the 
8 
anticipated change in sales mix in market to mostly residential projects 
9 
requires more capital spending because costs per customer connection are 
10 
higher for residential installations.    
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please describe the Customer Connections – Clean Choice Program. 
13 
A. 
The Clean Choice program provides alternatives to customers who want to 
14 
convert to a cleaner fuel in areas that meet customer density and minimum 
15 
customer connections requirements set by the program.  The program will 
16 
focus on more geothermal installations, and where geothermal is not an 
17 
option, customers will still have the option to make an environmentally 
18 
beneficial decision by conversion from oil to natural gas.  This program will 
19 
incorporate the web-based fuel switching calculator that is discussed in 
20 
KEDNY and KEDLI’s Future of Heat Panel testimony.     
21 


--- Page 59 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 56 of 110 
 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the Customer Connections –  AMR Program. 
2 
A. 
The Customer Connections-AMR Program includes the purchase and 
3 
installation of AMR equipment for new customer connection installations.  
4 
AMR equipment is not included in the Company’s Purchase Meters budget.  
5 
Based on the Company’s forecast, the Company will need to install between 
6 
7,000 and 8,000 AMRs per year during the Rate Year and Data Years at costs 
7 
of approximately $1.0 million per year.    
8 
  
9 
Q. 
Please describe the Gas System Reinforcement Program in the Customer 
10 
Connections category of spending. 
11 
A. 
The Gas System Reinforcements Program contains projects intended to ensure 
12 
that minimum system pressures are maintained throughout the gas network 
13 
during periods of peak demand.  The Company models peak demand based on 
14 
the sendout forecasts developed by Analytics, Modeling and Forecasting 
15 
(Company Witness Theodore Poe).  In response to demand for gas usage in its 
16 
service territory, KEDLI determined that it is necessary to complete several 
17 
projects to ensure its ability to meet peak requirements.  These reinforcement 
18 
projects are essential to ensure the Company can continue to serve growing 
19 
gas usage load.  Without such reinforcements, the Company estimates that 
20 
more than 32,000 existing customers could experience loss of service.    
21 


--- Page 60 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 57 of 110 
 
1 
KEDLI experienced new records for sendouts during the last two winter 
2 
heating seasons.  During the winter of 2017/2018, KEDLI recorded four of its 
3 
top ten sendout records, including a firm load record of 1,014,810 dekatherms 
4 
on January 6, 2018, when the average temperature was eight degrees 
5 
Fahrenheit.  During the winter of 2018/2019, KEDLI recorded two of its top 
6 
ten sendout records, including a firm load record of 1,029,616 dekatherms on 
7 
January 21, 2019, when the average temperature was thirteen degrees 
8 
Fahrenheit.  
9 
 
10 
As discussed in Section VII, below, and in the testimony of Company Witness 
11 
Elizabeth A. Arangio, additional capacity that is expected from the Williams’ 
12 
NESE project is critical to meeting this level of increasing demand.  Moreover, 
13 
the recent growth in peak sendout underscores the need to ensure that the 
14 
Company’s gas distribution facilities can maintain minimum system design 
15 
pressures during periods of peak demand.

Q. 
Please describe what is included in the CSC Program. 
1 
A. 
This program consists of construction work performed to accommodate third-
2 
party, municipal construction activity that could impact the integrity of the 
3 
Company’s natural gas facilities.  Typical third-party construction activities 
4 
that impact gas facilities include work on water, sewer, and drainage 
5 
infrastructure, street reconstruction, road realignment and bridge replacement.  
6 
State regulations and Company procedures require the replacement of eight 
7 
inch and smaller cast iron gas mains if roadway or underground construction 
8 
is being performed in such a way that would impact the integrity of the 
9 
Company’s mains.  Non-cast iron gas mains (i.e., steel and plastic) are not 
10 
subject to the same replacement regulations and are typically supported and 
11 
protected if not in direct conflict with third-party construction.  Direct 
12 
conflicts are addressed through relocation regardless of material type.   
13 
 
14 
Aging municipal infrastructure (buildings, schools, bridges, roadways, 
15 
transportation systems, water mains and sewer facilities) will require 
16 
significant upgrades in the coming years.  Over the last several years, there 
17 
had been an increase in the level of municipal construction activity, as 
18 
Superstorm Sandy and recent gas incidents focused attention on the state of 
19 
municipal infrastructure, but this work in KEDLI’s service area seems to have 
20 
plateaued.  The forecast cost for this program is approximately $9.8 million in 
21 


--- Page 64 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 61 of 110 
the Rate Year, $10.0 million in Data Year 1, $10.5 million in Data Year 2, and 
1 
$10.5 million in Data Year 3.    
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Are there opportunities to retire LPP during CSC projects? 
4 
A. 
Yes.  As part of the CSC Program, the Company looks to identify cost-
5 
effective opportunities to retire LPP when main replacements are required to 
6 
accommodate municipal construction.  CSC projects present opportunities to 
7 
perform safety and reliability upgrades on the Company’s infrastructure, the 
8 
costs of which can be offset by coordinating construction activities (shared 
9 
trenching and paving) and securing third party reimbursements.  Of the 
10 
approximately 15,096 linear feet (2.9 miles) of CSC main replacements 
11 
forecasted for FY 2020, approximately 10,560 linear feet (2 miles) of LPP is 
12 
expected to be retired.

Q. 
Please describe New York City’s joint bidding process and its potential 
13 
impact on CSC costs. 
14 
A. 
In 2014, New York State adopted a “joint bidding” requirement for public 
15 
works projects in cities with populations of one million or more (N.Y.S. 
16 
Assembly Bill A10021B) that will impact KEDLI’s CSC projects in New 
17 
York City (on the Rockaway Peninsula).  The City has not yet fully 
18 
implemented this process.  Currently, utilities impacted by New York City 
19 
public works projects are individually responsible for performing their own 
20 
relocation work, as well as negotiating all support and protect work directly 
21 


--- Page 66 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 63 of 110 
with the City’s contractor.  Under the “joint bidding” model, the City will bid 
1 
and manage the entire project.  If the City’s contractor is qualified by the 
2 
Company to work on its gas facilities, the contractor may perform the gas 
3 
relocation work.  If the City’s contractor is not gas qualified, KEDLI will 
4 
perform its own relocation work ahead of the planned project.  Each impacted 
5 
utility will also be responsible for a portion of the “shared costs” of work 
6 
performed by the City’s contractors, including, for example, maintaining the 
7 
construction site, establishing field offices, setting up transportation and 
8 
managing contracts and expenses.  It is unclear when the City will fully 
9 
implement the joint bidding process, but in recent months, the City has 
10 
increased discussions regarding implementation.  It is likely that the process 
11 
will be fully implemented within the next few years, but the scope of its 
12 
application is currently unknown.  The City’s workplan for FY 2019 and FY 
13 
2020 includes approximately 18 projects, across multiple boroughs and 
14 
utilities, using the joint bidding process.  Incremental costs of the joint bidding 
15 
process for FY 2019 and FY 2020 appear to be about $0.5 million for each 
16 
FY; however, the projects the City is jointly biding are not necessarily 
17 
representative of future project scopes that may use this process.  Because the 
18 
true cost implications on utilities of working through the City’s selected 
19 
contractors, as well as the impact of the shared costs contribution, remains to 
20 
be seen, the Company has not included additional costs in its CSC forecast.

Q.

15

16 
A.

17 
Main Rehabilitation Program, included in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate 
18 
Cases, that utilizes cured-in-place pipe lining (“CIP”) to cost effectively 
19

20

21 


--- Page 68 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 65 of 110 
reduce future joint leaks on treated pipeline segments for years.  As agreed in 
1

2

3

4

Data Years using CIP lining at a cost of approximately $6.5 million per year.     
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Why has the cost to CIP line one mile of pipe increased? 
8 
A. 
Issues arose in the execution of CIP lining program over the past three years 
9 
where an entire segment of main could not be continuously lined due to 
10 
configuration issues.  In these instances, work had to be stopped and small 
11 
sections of main had to be replaced between segments that could be lined.  As 
12 
a lesson learned, the Company adjusted it strategy to plan for replacement of 
13 
small segments that cannot be lined.  The proposed plan to line one mile per 
14 
year is more realistic and includes the time and funding to replace small 
15 
sections that cannot be lined.

11

12

13

18

19

20 
efficiently isolate sections of the distribution system while mitigating

2

3 
distribution system.  The Rate Year forecast of approximately $0.1 million 
4 
each year, adjusted for inflation in each of the Data Years.

Q. 
Please describe the Gas Control Training Simulator Project.  
6 
A. 
This program is for the purchase, design, and implementation of a real-time, 
7

8

9

10

11

12

13

14

15 
project represents KEDLI’s allocation of the total cost.

17 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Heater Installation and Pressure 
18 
Regulating Facilities Programs. 
19 
A. 
There are 28 natural gas heaters currently operating on the Company’s system.  
20 
Because high-pressure gas cools when reduced to a lower pressure, heaters are 
21 


--- Page 76 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 73 of 110 
required at pressure regulating stations to prevent freeze-ups that can impact 
1 
flow control devices.  In addition, cold gas temperatures can lead to reduced 
2 
pipe toughness and increased potential for frost heave and cold temperature-
3 
induced stresses.  The heater program adds new heaters (where required) and 
4 
replaces existing heaters that have reached the end of their useful lives.  The 
5 
Pressure Regulating Facilities category provides funding for replacement 
6 
and/or rebuilding and reconditioning of existing regulating and control 
7 
stations.   
8 
 
9 
The Pressure Regulating Facilities Program provides funding for replacement 
10 
and/or rebuilding and reconditioning of existing regulating and control 
11 
stations.  Pressure regulating facilities (or stations) are designed to control 
12 
system pressures and maintain continuity of supply during normal operating 
13 
conditions and during periods of peak gas demand.   
14 
 
15 
KEDLI has assessed regulating stations on its system, evaluating factors such 
16 
as pressure, location, including whether the station is in a FEMA 100 year or 
17 
500-year flood zone, and the number of dependent customers for each station.  
18 
In addition, the assessment considered station condition, including pipe 
19 
corrosion, location and type of overpressure protection, automation, condition 
20 
of vaults, vault covers, wall sleeves, piping vents and ladders.  The results of 
21 


--- Page 77 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 74 of 110 
the assessment were used to create an overall risk rating for each station that 
1 
serves as the basis for prioritizing projects in this program.  The program 
2 
includes full or partial replacement of existing stations, and storm hardening 
3 
upgrades where necessary. 
4 
 
5 
The investments in the Pressure Regulating Facilities Program do not cover 
6 
the special project capital improvements to specific stations that are separately 
7 
set forth in the investment plan and described below. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Please describe the pressure regulation special projects. 
10 
A. 
Pressure Regulation Special Projects are capital investments to address 
11 
reliability issues at specific stations that are separately budgeted and are not 
12 
included in any other blanket reliability programs.  These projects are 
13 
described in detail in the “Pressure Regulation Special Projects” data sheet 
14 
included in Exhibit __ (GIOP-5).  The projects target facilities that have the 
15 
highest potential customer impact.  Depending on the asset, these projects 
16 
include station replacement or rebuild, incorporation of odorization, gas 
17 
quality validation, pressure regulation, overpressure protection, storm 
18 
hardening improvements, and addition of process pre-heating equipment.   
19 
 
20 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the System Automation category. 
21 


--- Page 78 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 75 of 110 
A. 
This program will install updated Remote Terminal Units (“RTUs”) at 
1 
multiple city gate and regulator stations.  RTUs provide temperature, pressure 
2 
and flow data back to the Gas Control Room.  RTUs can also monitor gas 
3 
detectors and intrusion alarms and allow Gas Control Operators to adjust flow 
4 
and pressure set points at regulator stations.  The benefits include enhanced 
5 
calibration of network models from automation and telemetry data, improved 
6 
accuracy of network analysis, and enhanced ability to forecast the need for 
7 
capital reinforcements, which will lead to more efficient capital planning.  
8 
Automation allows Gas Control Operators to selectively close valves, raise or 
9 
lower pressures, and shut down take stations.  System alarms also alert Gas 
10 
Control Operators to system issues and allow quick pinpointing of the source.  
11 
 
12 
PHMSA regulations regarding Control Room Management require Operators 
13 
to ensure that “practices and procedures within their control rooms are 
14 
adequate to maintain pipeline safety and integrity.”  These rules indicate that 
15 
Operators should have telemetry to monitor pipelines, as it would increase 
16 
system awareness and enable a proactive response to abnormal operating 
17 
conditions.  The System Automation program complies with these regulations 
18 
by providing for increased deployment of telemetry on the Company’s system. 
19 
 
20 


--- Page 79 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 76 of 110 
Currently, KEDLI’s pressure regulation stations are equipped with some form 
1 
of telemetry, but only 96 percent of the system has full remote control of the 
2 
regulators.  Additionally, some of the telemetry equipment was installed years 
3 
ago and the technology has become obsolete.  KEDLI’s System Automation 
4 
program adds automation capabilities to low pressure regulator stations and 
5 
updates RTU equipment and communications capabilities.  This program will 
6 
provide enhanced ability to monitor system performance and remotely adjust 
7 
pressures on the gas system.   
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Gas System Reliability – Gas 
10 
Planning/Remote Controlled Valve (“RCV”) Program. 
11 
A. 
This program includes capital projects required to maintain system minimum 
12 
pressures on the gas network in the event of an abnormal operating condition 
13 
(failure involving a regulator station, gate station, critical main or other major 
14 
pressure facility on the system).  Gas System Planning ensures that customers 
15 
continue to have reliable service and that no customers experience 
16 
interruptions because of an unplanned outage of a facility under normal winter 
17 
conditions.  The program also involves the installation of additional RCVs on 
18 
transmission pipelines to improve emergency response capability and reduce 
19 
the risk of gas releases.  In the event of a pipeline failure, RCVs allow control 
20 
room operators to stop the flow of gas and to remotely isolate and shut down a 
21 


--- Page 80 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 77 of 110 
portion of the system.  Currently, most transmission pipelines can only be shut 
1 
down using manually-controlled isolation valves, which can take longer to 
2 
close and result in a larger customer impact.  Improving response time 
3 
through the expanded deployment of RCVs reduces the quantity of gas 
4 
released and can limit the harm to the public and property.   
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Please provide examples of Gas Planning Reliability projects. 
7 
A. 
Examples of Gas Planning Reliability projects include: eliminating 
8 
distribution systems fed by a single district regulator or main, integrating 
9 
distribution systems with the same operating pressures through pipeline 
10 
connections, expanding supply diversity, and projects targeting areas of the 
11 
system where large numbers of customers would experience a service 
12 
interruption if a single gas facility became inoperable when the average daily 
13 
temperature is five degrees Fahrenheit. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Why does the Company propose to include installation of RCVs in the 
16 
capital investment plan? 
17 
A. 
The PHMSA regulations promulgated in response to the Pipeline Safety Act 
18 
of 2011 will mandate the installation of additional RCVs.  But even in the 
19 
absence of the PHMSA regulations, investment in RCVs is prudent given the 
20 
safety and reliability benefits.  As highlighted by recent industry events, there 
21 


--- Page 81 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 78 of 110 
are significant operational benefits associated with the increased deployment 
1 
of RCVs, such as enhanced pipeline shutdown capabilities. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Water Intrusion Program. 
4 
A. 
The Water Intrusion program is designed to address water entering the gas 
5 
distribution system, resulting in main obstructions, poor pressure and/or 
6 
freezing customer services.  This program targets the retirement of LPP that is 
7 
susceptible to water intrusion but is not prioritized for replacement under the 
8 
Main Replacement programs because of the absence of leaks and/or historical 
9 
leak repair activity. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please describe the Storm Hardening – Remote Service Shutoff Valves 
12 
Program. 
13 
A. 
In the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the Company included a 
14 
proposal for a Storm Hardening Program to begin installing remote service 
15 
shutoff valves on KEDLI’s approximately 60,000 gas services within the 
16 
FEMA’s designated flood zones over five years beginning in CY 2017.  
17 
Because there was no viable shutoff valve mechanism commercially available 
18 
for use on the system, the Company was unable to timely launch this program.  
19 
The devices became available from the manufacturer during February of 
20 
2019, and the Company plans to begin installations in FY 2020.  Because of 
21 


--- Page 82 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 79 of 110 
the significant benefits of these devices, the Company is renewing its proposal 
1 
for this program from FY 2020 through FY 2024. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What are the benefits and costs of this program?      
4 
A. 
 The remote shutoff valves add a layer of protection for services that are 
5 
especially susceptible to storm surge and flooding that could cause over-
6 
pressurization of the gas facilities connecting customers’ premises.  The 
7 
impacts of recent severe storms (i.e., Superstorm Sandy) demonstrate the need 
8 
for the Company to harden its infrastructure to provide greater protection from 
9 
future storms.  These valves will operate on a fixed communication network 
10 
that will allow for remote operation and monitoring.  Automated valves stop 
11 
the flow of gas as soon as flooding is detected.  This will prevent regulator 
12 
over-pressurization and stop gas from flowing to premises with damaged 
13 
equipment and/or extinguished pilot lights, mitigating the risk of a potential 
14 
incident.  Automated valves also provide a real-time count of services 
15 
impacted by flooding to inform the Company’s storm response about the 
16 
resources needed to restore the affected customers expeditiously.  Lastly, in 
17 
areas where flooding prevents physical access to valves and regulators, remote 
18 
shut-off valves will allow the Company to interrupt only those services 
19 
impacted by flooding, which could spare entire neighborhoods or larger areas 
20 
from losing gas service because of access issues.  Installation of the 
21 


--- Page 83 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 80 of 110 
communication networks through which the valves operate can also be 
1 
leveraged in the future for additional smart devices, such as methane detectors 
2 
that are being developed.    
3 
 
4 
The Company is proposing to ramp-up the program in FY 2020 and the Rate 
5 
Year, installing approximately 1,000 valves in FY 2020 and approximately 
6 
9,000 valves in the Rate Year, with the bulk of the communications network 
7 
systems in the Rate Year as well.  The Company intends to install 
8 
approximately 16,500 valves per year in each of the Data Years.  Program 
9 
costs in the Rate Year are approximately $15.6 million.     
10

20 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s LNG programs. 
21 


--- Page 84 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 81 of 110 
A. 
KEDLI maintains on-system supply through its LNG facility at the Holtsville 
1 
LNG Plant.  This plant is a critical component of KEDLI’s gas supply 
2 
portfolio and gas operating network and plays a critical role in meeting peak 
3 
demand.  The Holtsville LNG Plant has one storage tank and is capable of 
4 
supplying 100 million cubic feet of gas per day.  Refilling the tank is 
5 
accomplished through liquefaction during the summer period when gas 
6 
supplies are available and less expensive.  The liquefaction system can refill at 
7 
a rate of about 6 million cubic feet of gas per day and it can take up to100 
8 
days to refill the tank.  The Holtsville LNG Plant is a cost-effective means of 
9 
meeting peak demand, because gas is liquified during the summer months 
10 
when prices are lower and stored for use in the winter during peak demand 
11 
conditions.  KEDLI’s various LNG capital programs are necessary to ensure 
12 
the continued safe, reliable operation of the Holtsville LNG Plant.   
13

Q. 
What is included in the Company’s Learning and Development (“L&D”) 
4 
Materials Tools and Equipment Program? 
5 
A. 
L&D has a limited stock of tools for use in training and has relied on tools 
6 
borrowed from Field Operations to supplement its supply and conduct 
7 
required training of in-house and contractor employees.  With workload and 
8 
training requirements increasing, Field Operations can no longer spare tools 
9 
and equipment sufficient for training purposes, and the limited stock of 
10 
available tools is beginning to deteriorate.  The proposed L&D Materials 
11 
Tools and Equipment Program will purchase new tools and equipment for 
12 
training purposes.  The program will follow an innovative approach whereby 
13 
employees will train on new tools during the annual training sessions at the 
14 
beginning of each year, and then the new tools will be sent into the field for 
15 
use by the newly-trained employees.  Following annual training, Field 
16 
Operations will send its in-use tools back to L&D for refurbishment and use in 
17 
supplemental training throughout the year.  This tool swap (i) enables more 
18 
effective training on the equipment that will be used in the field, (ii) enhances 
19 
the quality of tools used in the field, and (iii) ensures that L&D has a 
20 
sufficient inventory of tools to conduct supplemental training during the year 
21 


--- Page 89 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 86 of 110 
after annual training has concluded.  The program also increases the useful 
1 
life of field tools that are swapped back into L&D for refurbishment that 
2 
otherwise may have failed earlier in the field due to continuous use. 
3

F. Capitalization Policy Change – Main Installation 
1 
 
2 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing any changes to its capitalization policies for 
3 
main installation? 
4 
A. 
Yes.  Accounting for installation of segments of main under 50 feet in length 
5 
is currently expensed by the Company.  However, this threshold is arbitrary; 
6 
moreover, there is no similar threshold for main replacement in Niagara 
7 
Mohawk’s service territory.  Applicable accounting principles and regulations 
8 
permit capitalization of new main installations, and there is no minimum 
9 
threshold.  The Company is proposing to remove the 50-foot threshold and 
10 
capitalize all main installation activities in accordance with the FERC Chart of 
11 
Accounts – Gas – Operating Expense Instructions, Accounts 367 Mains and 
12 
376 Mains.  This change will standardize the accounting treatment of these 
13 
work activities between National Grid’s New York gas distribution 
14 
companies.  As discussed in the testimony of the Revenue Requirements 
15 
Panel, the Company has normalized its Historic Test Year to reflect the 
16 
change from expense to capital for distribution and transmission main 
17 
installation, and the Company’s capital investment forecasts also reflect this 
18 
capitalization change.    
19

Data Year 2 and $6.8 million in Data Year 3.  These facilities are National 
6 
Grid Service Company investments and the costs are set forth in the Revenue 
7 
Requirements Panel’s Exhibit __ (RRP-11), Workpapers to Exhibit RRP-3, 
8 
Schedule 9, Workpapers 4, 7, 10, and 13.  The large facilities investment 
9 
projects include: 
10 
 
11 
• Replacement of the Hicksville Office and Operating Site:  National 
12 
Grid currently occupies 237,000 square feet of the 515,000 square feet 
13 
available at this site.  PSEG-LI occupies the remainder of the site.  
14 
National Grid plans to relocate its operations to a 100,000 square foot 
15 
site and, in so doing, achieve savings through a reduction in the total 
16 
footprint and the elimination of inefficiencies that arise from the joint 
17 
occupation of the current site.  Forecast capital expenditures associated 
18 
with the relocation for National Grid Service Company total 
19 
approximately $3.1 million in the Gap Period, $19 million in the Rate 
20 


--- Page 98 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 95 of 110 
Year, $0.5 million in Data Year 1, $0.5 million in Data Year 2, and 
1 
$0.5 million in Data Year 3. 
2 
National Grid has been sharing this site with PSEG-LI since it 
3 
assumed responsibility for the administration of the Long Island Power 
4 
Authority’s transmission and distribution systems in 2014.  Sharing 
5 
major operations facilities is not optimal for the Company from an 
6 
efficiency, safety or security perspective.  Moreover, sharing a major 
7 
operations site makes it more difficult to promote and brand National 
8 
Grid to the public.  In addition, it creates issues with respect to 
9 
recruiting, training and retaining employees.  Relocating National 
10 
Grid’s operations to a more efficient, Company-only site is clearly 
11 
beneficial from a company, employee, and customer perspective. 
12 
• Brooklyn, NY Office Relocation:  National Grid will relocate existing 
13

14 
reduce the existing footprint.  National Grid is examining potential 
15 
locations for this relocation at this time.  However, a move to a smaller 
16 
footprint will reduce costs and enable the Company to make more 
17 
efficient use of space.  As part of this plan, KEDNY’s call center, 
18 
customer office, and Sustainability Hub will remain at the current

Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 97 of 110 
VI. 
Gas O&M Expenses 
1 
Q. 
Please summarize the Panel’s testimony regarding the costs of operating 
2 
the gas system.  
3 
A. 
The Panel addresses major expenses associated with operating the Company’s 
4 
gas delivery system, and incremental O&M expenses the Company expects to 
5 
incur in the Rate Year and Data Years.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please generally describe the nature of the Company’s gas system O&M 
8 
expenses. 
9 
A. 
O&M expenses relate to work performed to provide customer support, 
10 
respond to emergencies, perform safety inspections and other compliance 
11 
activities, restore service and maintain the life of capital assets.  The Company 
12 
has a significant maintenance program to ensure that system assets are utilized 
13 
to their fullest potential life expectancy.  As gas facilities age, maintenance 
14 
costs increase.  These costs include increased volume of repairs, more 
15 
significant repair work, and increased emergency work.  These expenditures 
16 
are required to prevent failure and maintain the life of the assets until 
17 
replacement occurs.  The O&M workload is also increasing to keep pace with 
18 
expanding inspection and testing requirements from new regulatory and 
19 
pipeline safety mandates.  Finally, the Company’s O&M programs are 
20 
designed to maintain the service commitments in its gas safety performance 
21 


--- Page 101 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 98 of 110 
metrics, which cover various aspects of its performance in the areas of 
1 
reliability and safety, including metrics measuring pipeline replacement, 
2 
emergency response, leak management, and damage prevention.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
How does the projected Rate Year non-labor expense level compare to 
5 
the Historic Test Year non-labor expenses for operating the gas system?  
6 
A. 
As shown on Exhibit __ (GIOP-7), the Company projects its incremental Rate 
7 
Year non-labor O&M expense to be approximately $15.496 million for gas 
8 
operations and gas safety programs, which is approximately 13.7 percent 
9 
greater than its adjusted O&M expense for the Historic Test Year.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
What are the projected Rate Year O&M incremental labor 
12 
requirements?   
13 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit __ (GIOP-8), the Company also proposed to hire 75.3 
14 
total incremental FTEs in the Rate Year to support its O&M and gas safety 
15 
program requirements.  The costs for these FTEs are supported by the 
16 
Revenue Requirements Panel and presented in Exhibit___(RRP-3), Schedule 
17 
27. 
18 
19 


--- Page 102 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 99 of 110 
Q. 
Please summarize the projected incremental O&M expenditures.   
1 
A. 
Increases in O&M expense are primarily driven by (i) the Company’s 
2 
increasing capital investments and increased costs for executing the 
3 
Company’s capital plan, (ii) changes to Company’s O&M workload, and (iii) 
4 
initiatives the Company is undertaking in the Rate Year to address new or 
5 
expanding safety requirements and to implement lessons learned from recent 
6 
industry gas system incidents.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
What is the Company doing to manage its O&M costs? 
9 
A. 
The Company has implemented various initiatives to reduce its O&M 
10 
expenses, including:  
11 
• Increasing the use of scheduled O&M work appointments to reduce 
12 
multiple unproductive field visits to complete work.   
13 
• Coordinating O&M activities required at each premise so that multiple 
14 
maintenance requirements can be completed during a single visit.  
15 
• Increasing the use of coring and low-dig technology, reducing debris 
16 
removal and paving restoration costs associated with smaller roadway 
17 
excavations. 
18 
• Modifying shift schedules to more efficiently respond to higher leak 
19 
volumes. 
20 


--- Page 103 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 100 of 110 
• Exploring new work management systems to optimize dispatch of 
1 
resources.  
2 
Notably, during CY 2018, the Company undertook a re-organization of its 
3 
Gas Business Unit to optimize the level of managers and FTEs to arrive at a 
4 
more efficient balance of personnel.   
5
